Viscosity Solutions of a class of Second Order Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman Equations in the Wasserstein Space
Hang Cheung, Ho Man Tai, Jinniao Qiu

Abstract
This paper is devoted to solving a class of second order Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman (HJB) equations in the Wasserstein space, associated with mean field control problems involving common noise. The well-posedness of viscosity solutions to the HJB equation under a new notion is established under general assumptions on the coefficients. Our approach adopts the smooth metric developed by Bayraktar, Ekren, and Zhang [Proc. Amer. Math. Soc. (2023)] as our gauge function for the purpose of smooth variational principle used in the proof of comparison theorem. Further estimates and regularity of the metric, including a novel second order derivative estimate with respect to the measure variable, are derived in order to ensure the uniqueness and existence.



Keywords:
mean field type control, Wasserstein space, HJB equation, viscosity solutions, Bellman equation, comparison theorem, Ekeland’s variational principle.

Mathematics Subject Classification (2020):
49L25, 35Q93, 35B51, 58E30.

1
Introduction

The primary objective of this paper is to establish the well-posedness of viscosity solutions for second order Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman (HJB) equations, which arise from control problems in the space of probability measures over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, under a newly proposed notion (see Definition
6.1
and Remark
6.2
for the rationale behind this definition). This is achieved under mild assumptions on the coefficients, as detailed in Section 2.3. The main HJB equation of interest is given by:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &-\partial_{t}u(t,\mu)=\dfrac{1}{2}\textup{tr}\Big[\sigma^{0}(t)[\sigma^{0}(t)]^{\top}\mathcal{H}u(t,\mu)\Big]\\
&+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{a\in A}\Bigg\{f(t,x,\mu,a)+b(t,x,\mu,a)\cdot\partial_{\mu}u(t,\mu)(x)\\
&\hskip 55.0pt+\dfrac{1}{2}\textup{tr}\Big(\sigma(t,x,a)\big[\sigma(t,x,a)\big]^{\top}\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}u(t,\mu)(x)\Big)\Bigg\}\mu(dx)\,,\hskip 4.0pt\text{ $(t,\mu)\in[0,T)\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$};\\
&u(T,\mu)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(x,\mu)\mu(dx)\,,\hskip 10.0pt\text{$\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$}.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.1)

In the above, the operator
\mathcal{H}
is defined as in
[
5
]
, that is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}u(\mu):=\frac{d^{2}}{dw^{2}}u\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big($}}}}}(I_{d}+w)_{\sharp}\mu\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big)$}}}}}\Big|_{w=0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},

(1.2)

where
(I_{d}+w)_{\sharp}\mu
is the pushforward measure associated with map
x\mapsto x+w
; and
\frac{d}{dw}
is the usual gradient operator on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Indeed, for sufficiently smooth functions
u:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
, it holds that (see Lemma
2.5
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}u(\mu)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}u(\mu)(x)\mu(dx)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial^{2}_{\mu}u(\mu)(x,\tilde{x})\mu(dx)\mu(d\tilde{x}),

and thus
\mathcal{H}
may be viewed as the second order differential operator with respect to the measure variable in a “weaker” sense. We refer readers to Section 2 for notations used in the above. This HJB equation characterizes the value function for the following mean field type control problem with common noise:

\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{T}f\Big(s,X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}^{W^{0}}_{X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha}},\alpha_{s}\Big)ds+g\Big(X_{T}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}_{X_{T}^{t,\xi,\alpha}}^{W^{0}}\Big)\Bigg]

subject to the dynamics

X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha}=\xi+\int_{t}^{s}b\Big(r,X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}_{X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha}}^{W^{0}},\alpha_{r}\Big)dr+\int_{t}^{s}\sigma(r,X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\alpha_{r})dW_{r}+\int_{t}^{s}\sigma^{0}(r)dW^{0}_{r}\,,

where
W
and
W^{0}
are independent Wiener processes representing idiosyncratic and common noises respectively; and
\mathbb{P}_{X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha}}^{W^{0}}
denotes the conditional distribution of
X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha}
given
W^{0}
.

The theory of mean field type (McKean-Vlasov type) control problems and related mean field games, initiated independently by Lasry and Lions
[
32
]
and Caines, Huang, and Malhame
[
28
]
, have experienced significant growth and development over the past decade. Mean field type control problems introduce measure dependence in both the state processes and cost functionals, which allows the modeling of large-scale systems with agents interacting symmetrically. Readers are referred to the comprehensive monographs such as Carmona and Delaure
[
14
,
15
]
, Bensoussan, Frehse and Yam
[
7
]
, and the references therein for more exhaustive discussions.

In standard control theory, two primary approaches are prominent: the Pontryagin maximum principle and the
HJB
equation. The Pontryagin maximum principle aims to establish necessary conditions that characterize the optimal control, often relying on solving a system of mean field forward-backward stochastic differential equations. Extensive research has been conducted in this area and interested readers can refer to Andersson and Djehiche
[
1
]
, Li
[
33
]
, Buckdahn, Djehiche, and Li
[
10
]
, Carmona and Delaure
[
13
]
, Bensoussan, Tai, and Yam
[
8
]
, Bensoussan, Wong, Yam, and Yuan
[
9
]
, Gangbo, Mészáros, Mou, and Zhang
[
27
]
, Mou and Zhang
[
35
]
, Chassagneux, Crisan, and Delarue
[
16
]
, Bayraktar, Cosso, and Pham
[
3
]
, Bayraktar, Alekos, and Prakash
[
2
]
, among others.
The latter approach links the solvability of the control problems to HJB equations. It is widely accepted that, apart from specific cases like standard linear quadratic scenarios, classical solutions to the HJB equations are generally hard to obtain. Because of this, Crandall, Ishii, and Lions
[
19
]
, Lions
[
34
]
introduced the concept of viscosity solution, which allows solutions in a much broader sense. For viscosity solutions in the Euclidean space or Hilbert space, readers are referred to Crandall and Lions
[
20
,
21
]
, Cannarsa and Soner
[
11
]
, and so on.

In the context of the Wasserstein space, there are several notable works focusing on viscosity solutions of HJB equations. Pham and Wei
[
37
]
introduced a dynamic programming principle tailored for cases where the control is adapted to the filtration generated by the common noise only. Due to the lack of local compactness in the infinite-dimensional Wasserstein space, they lifted their resulting HJB equation to the Hilbert space of random variables to establish both the uniqueness and existence of the viscosity solution. To bypass this lifting procedure, Wu and Zhang
[
44
]
introduced a new viscosity solution concept, departing from the traditional Crandall-Lions’ definition. Their approach required the maximum/minimum condition to hold on a compact subset of the Wasserstein space instead of just a local neighborhood, enabling the development of a comprehensive theory of viscosity solutions. In an attempt to align with the original Crandall-Lions’ definition and address local compactness challenges in the Wasserstein space, Cosso, Gozzi, Kharroubi, Pham, and Rosestolato
[
17
]
utilized the Borwein-Preiss generalization of Ekeland’s variational principle. This principle addresses the local compactness concerns by showing that a small perturbation of the value function with a smooth gauge function can attain its maximum or minimum.
Through this, they tried to extend the Crandall-Lions’ definition of viscosity solutions to the Wasserstein space.

The above, excluding
[
37
]
, pertain to first order HJB equations in the Wasserstein spaces. The presence of common noise induces the second order
L
-derivative in these equations, which has garnered significant attention in recent times. Bayraktar, Ekren, and Zhang
[
5
]
made use of the observation (see the operator
\mathcal{H}
in (
1.2
)) that the second order
L
-derivative appears in the form of integration and is actually finite-dimensional. Together with the Fourier-Wasserstein metric employed in
[
40
]
, they successfully established Ishii’s lemma in the Wasserstein space. This accomplishment allowed a direct comparison of second order equations, rather than relying on finite-dimensional approximation methods as outlined in
[
17
]
. Consequently, it broadened the scope to include a wider array of second order equations beyond those induced solely from control problems like HJB equations. However, their result required stringent regularity assumptions imposed on the coefficients, because of the utilization of the Fourier-Wasserstein metric. On top of the finite-dimensional observation for the second order derivative, Samuel, Joe and Benjamin
[
22
]
established the well-posedness of viscosity solutions for a class of semi-linear Hamilton-Jacobi equations (which might not arise from control problems) by using some delicate estimates for a sequence of finite-dimensional approximating PDEs and mollification techniques. Nonetheless, they were concerned only about the space of probability measures over the torus. See also Bayraktar, Ekren and Zhang
[
6
]
for the convergence rate. More recently, a new notion of viscosity solutions has been proposed by Touzi, Zhang and Zhou
[
46
]
accompanied by the establishment of existence and comparison principles under the sole assumption of Lipschitz continuity. The main feature of their new notion is its incorporation of an additional singular component in the test function, enabling the treatment of second order derivatives of test functions without invoking Ishii’s lemma. Their new notion allows them to handle second order equations with both drift and volatility controls.
Nevertheless, this notion is different from the Crandall-Lions’ definition in the sense that it allows a nonsmooth component in the test function.
In contrast to the literature referenced, the primary contribution of our article involves considering the HJB equation incorporating the second order measure derivative under mild assumptions. Furthermore, our approach to viscosity solutions is intrinsic
(in the sense that we do not have to lift the equations to Hilbert spaces)
, and we extend our analysis to allow the state space to encompass the entire space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
rather than being confined to a torus.

To establish the uniqueness of the viscosity solution to (
1.1
), a pivotal idea is to seek a suitable comparison function with adequate regularity and favorable estimates on its derivatives, for the purpose of the comparison theorem. Drawing inspiration from
[
17
]
, we choose a gauge-type function to construct the comparison function via the Borwein-Preiss variational principle. However, the gauge function used in
[
17
]
encounters difficulties when dealing with the second order
L
-derivative, particularly in addressing mean field-type control problems involving common noise. To resolve this, we adopt the Gaussian regularized sliced Wasserstein distance proposed in
[
4
]
to compose our gauge function. This provides a metric over the space of high-dimensional probability distributions using their one-dimensional projections, offering the advantage of explicitly deriving the optimal transport map, which facilitates estimates of the derivatives. We observe that when the state and the law of the first order
L
-derivative of this metric move together in a deterministic direction (which is captured by the operator
\mathcal{H}
, see Lemma
4.7
), the gauge function behaves linearly with respect to the perturbed direction, enabling the estimation of the second order
L
-derivative of this gauge function in a “weaker” sense. As this gauge function will be used as a test function, we have to define a customized function space which is
{PC}_{1}^{1,2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
(see Definition
2.4
) as our set of test functions. Then, we perturb the HJB equation and mollify the coefficients as in
[
17
]
, together with the favorable estimates derived for the gauge function to complete the comparison theorem, as presented in Theorem
6.3
. By the specific form of the optimal transport map in
\mathbb{R}
, we provide further regularity results of this metric for the purpose of the relaxed Itô formula in Theorem
2.6
, which is crucial in establishing the existence of viscosity solutions. Moreover, this gauge function lacks standard linear or quadratic growth, which requires a more subtle analysis in the proof of the existence of viscosity solution compared to standard test functions.
We note that our proposed definition of the viscosity supersolution in Definition
6.1
is stronger than that of Crandall-Lions. It aims to overcome technical difficulties arising from the supremum taken in equation (
1.1
), which leads to scarce choice of test functions in the proof of the comparison for the supersolution. The cost functional with a fixed control is used to construct the test function for supersolutions as in
[
17
]
, but with the domain of
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times A)
rather than
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The strong dependency of the initial random variable and the control makes it hard to use only the Crandall-Lions’ definition to draw the conclusion. See Remark
6.2
for more details.

This paper is organized as follows: Section 2 covers the foundational elements: the control problem set-up, some definitions of our tools, Itô formulas and the standing assumptions. In Section 3, we explore fundamental properties of the value function and introduce the dynamic programming principle. Moving to Section 4, we provide some estimates of the Gaussian regularized sliced Wasserstein distance and state the Borwein-Preiss variational principle. In Section 5, we outline the finite-dimensional approximation scheme of the value function, drawing inspiration from
[
17
]
. Section 6 is dedicated to proving the existence and uniqueness of the viscosity solution, which stands as the value function of our control problem.

2
Preliminary

2.1
Probabilistic Setting and Notations

Fix a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
of the form
(\Omega^{0}\times\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{0}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{1})
. The space
(\Omega^{0},\mathcal{F}^{0},\mathbb{P}^{0})
supports a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion
W^{0}
which we regard as the common noise. For
(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1})
, it is of the form
(\tilde{\Omega}^{1}\times\hat{\Omega}^{1},\mathcal{G}\otimes\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{1},\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\otimes\hat{\mathbb{P}}^{1})
. On
(\hat{\Omega}^{1},\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{1},\hat{\mathbb{P}}^{1})
, there lives a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion
W
which we regard as the idiosyncratic noise, whereas
(\tilde{\Omega}^{1},\mathcal{G},\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{1})
is where the initial random variables live. We assume that the probability space
(\tilde{\Omega}^{1},\mathcal{G},\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{1})
is rich enough to support all probability laws in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, i.e., for any probability law
\mu
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there exists
X\in\tilde{\Omega}^{1}
such that the law of
X
, denoted by
\mathcal{L}(X)
, is
\mu
.

We write
\omega\in\Omega
as
\omega=(\omega^{0},\omega^{1})
, and regard the Brownian motions
W(\omega)=W(\omega^{1})
,
W^{0}(\omega)=W^{0}(\omega^{0})
. We denote by
\mathbb{E}
the expectation under
\mathbb{P}
and by
\mathbb{E}^{0}
(resp.
\mathbb{E}^{1}
) the expectation under
\mathbb{P}^{0}
(resp.
\mathbb{P}^{1}
). Also, we set
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{s})_{s\geq 0}:=(\sigma(W^{0}_{r})_{0\leq r\leq s}\vee\sigma(W_{r})_{0\leq r\leq s}\vee\mathcal{G})_{s\geq 0}
,
\mathbb{F}^{t}=(\mathcal{F}_{s}^{t})_{s\geq 0}:=(\sigma(W_{r}^{0})_{0\leq r\leq s}\vee\sigma(W_{r\vee t}-W_{t})_{0\leq r\leq s}\vee\mathcal{G})_{s\geq 0}
,
\mathbb{F}^{W^{0}}=(\mathcal{F}_{s}^{W^{0}})_{s\geq 0}:=(\sigma(W_{r}^{0}))_{0\leq r\leq s}
, and
\mathbb{F}^{1}=(\mathcal{F}_{s}^{1})_{s\geq 0}:=(\sigma(W_{s})\vee\mathcal{G})_{{0\leq r\leq s}}
. Without loss of generality, we assume they are
\mathbb{P}
-complete.

Let
A
be a compact subset of the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
equipped with the distance
d_{A}
. Let
t>0
and denote by
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}_{t}
) the set of
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable processes (resp.
\mathbb{F}^{t}
-progressively measurable processes) on
\Omega
valued in
A
. Note that both
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}_{t}
are separable metric spaces endowed with the Krylov distance
\Delta(\alpha,\beta):=\mathbb{E}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big[$}}}}}\int_{0}^{T}d_{A}(\alpha_{r},\beta_{r})dr\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big]$}}}}}
. Denote by
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}
) the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}_{t}
). We assume that
(\Omega^{0},\mathcal{F}^{0},\mathbb{P}^{0})
is the canonical space, i.e.,
\Omega^{0}=C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the set of continuous functions from
\mathbb{R}_{+}
into
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For any
\omega^{0},\hat{\omega}^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
,
r\in[0,T]
, we set

\displaystyle\hat{\omega}^{0}\otimes_{r}\omega^{0}(s):=\hat{w}^{0}(s)\mathbbm{1}_{[0,r)}(s)+\big[\hat{w}^{0}(r)+\omega^{0}(s)-\omega^{0}(r)\big]\mathbbm{1}_{[r,T]}(s).

Let
r\in[0,T]
, for
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s.
\tilde{\omega}^{0}
,
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t}
for some
t\in[0,T]
, we define:

\displaystyle\alpha^{r,\tilde{\omega}^{0}}(\omega^{0},\omega^{1}):=\alpha(\tilde{\omega}^{0}\otimes_{r}\omega^{0},\omega^{1}).

For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we use
\left|x\right|
to denote the Euclidean norm of
x
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
x_{i}
(sometimes we also use
(x)_{i}
or
[x]_{i}
) to denote the
i
-th component of
x
,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
(or simply the
x\cdot y
for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
) to denote the standard scalar product on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x^{1},x^{2},\ldots,x^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we use
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{dn}
to denote
\overline{x}:=(x^{1},x^{2},\ldots,x^{n})
. For any matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we use
\textup{tr}M:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}M_{ii}
to denote its trace,
M^{\top}
to denote its transpose, and
|M|:=\left[\operatorname{tr}\left(MM^{\top}\right)\right]^{1/2}

=\left(\sum_{i,j}^{d}\left|M_{ij}\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}
to denote the Frobenius norm of
M
. If
M^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is another matrix, we use
M^{0;\top}
to denote the transpose of
M^{0}
. We denote the identity matrix over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
I_{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. If
x\in\mathbb{R}
is a scalar variable, the notation
\partial_{x}h\in\mathbb{R}
means the usual partial derivative of the scalar function
h
with respect to
x
; if
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a vector variable, then
\partial_{x}h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
means the gradient vector of
h
.

Remark 2.1
.

Here we explain a little bit about the definition of
\mathbb{F}^{t}
. It makes no technical difference whether we consider the control sets which is progressively measurable with respect to
\mathbb{F}^{t}=(\mathcal{F}_{s}^{t})_{s\geq 0}:=(\sigma(W_{r}^{0})_{0\leq r\leq s}\vee\sigma(W_{r\vee t}-W_{t})_{0\leq r\leq s}\vee\mathcal{G})_{s\geq 0}
or
\mathring{\mathbb{F}}^{t}=(\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{s}^{t})_{s\geq 0}:=(\sigma(W^{0}_{r\vee t}-W^{0}_{t})_{0\leq r\leq s}\vee\sigma(W_{r\vee t}-W_{t})_{0\leq r\leq s}\vee\mathcal{G})_{s\geq 0}
. All the arguments in this article remain valid with slight or no modification. In fact, denoting
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
to be the set of all
\mathring{\mathbb{F}}^{t}
-progressively measurable processes taking value in
A
, and the value function
\mathring{V}(t,\xi):=\sup_{\alpha\in\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{t}}J(t,\xi,\alpha)
(see (
2.4
) for the definition), we actually have

\displaystyle\mathring{V}(t,\xi)=V(t,\xi),

where
V
is defined in (
2.5
). This can be seen from the following: First of all, as
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{t}\subset\mathcal{A}_{t}
, it follows that
\mathring{V}(t,\xi)\leq V(t,\xi)
. To show the other direction of the inequality, let
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t}
, then

\displaystyle J(t,\xi,\alpha)=

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{T}f(s,X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}^{W^{0}}_{X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha}},\alpha_{s})ds+g\Big(X_{T}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}_{X_{T}^{t,\xi,\alpha}}^{W^{0}}\Big)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{E}^{0}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{T}f(s,X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}^{W^{0}}_{X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha}},\alpha_{s})ds+g\Big(X_{T}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}_{X_{T}^{t,\xi,\alpha}}^{W^{0}}\Big)\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t}^{0}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{E}^{0}[J(t,\xi,\alpha^{t,\omega^{0}})]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{E}^{0}[\mathring{V}(t,\xi)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\mathring{V}(t,\xi).

Taking supremum with respect to
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t}
we conclude the desired equality.

The requirement that the controls are adapted to
\mathcal{G}
for every
s\geq 0
is a bit technical. The condition that the controls have to be adapted to
\mathcal{G}
is used in the existence theorem. Because of the nonlocal nature of the HJB equations in the Wasserstein space, assuming that
\varphi
is our test function, in the proof of existence of viscosity solution we can only conclude that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi(t_{0},\mu_{0})+\sup_{\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{t}}\mathbb{E}\Big\{f(t_{0},\xi,\mu_{0},\alpha^{\prime})+\partial_{\mu}\varphi(t_{0},\mu_{0})(\xi)\cdot b(t_{0},\xi,\mu_{0},\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}\left[\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}\varphi(t_{0},\mu_{0})(\xi)\sigma(t_{0},\xi,\alpha^{\prime})\sigma(t_{0},\xi,\alpha^{\prime})^{\top}\right]\Big\}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}\left[\mathcal{H}\varphi(t_{0},\mu_{0})\sigma^{0}(t_{0})\sigma^{0}(t_{0})^{\top}\right]\leq\text{(or }\geq)\ 0,

(2.1)

where
\mathcal{L}(\xi)=\mu_{0}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{t}
is the set of
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{t}
-measurable random variables. The condition of requiring the control set to be adapted to
\mathcal{G}
makes the controls rich enough such that we can choose controls to make the above equivalent to

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi(t_{0},\mu_{0})+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{a\in A}\Bigg\{f(t_{0},x,\mu_{0},a)+b(t_{0},x,\mu_{0},a)\cdot\partial_{\mu}\varphi(t_{0},\mu_{0})(x)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}\Big[\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}\varphi(t_{0},\mu_{0})(x)\sigma(t_{0},x,a)[\sigma(t_{0},x,a)]^{\top}\Big]\Bigg\}\mu_{0}(dx)+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}\Big\{\mathcal{H}\varphi(t_{0},\mu_{0})\sigma^{0}(t_{0})[\sigma^{0}(t_{0})]^{\top}\Big\}\leq\text{(or }\geq)\ 0,

(2.2)

see Theorem
6.2
.

2.2
Sense of Differentiability in the Wasserstein Space

We introduce over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
the space of probability measures
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and its subset
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
consisting of those with finite
p
-th moment for
p\geq 1
. The space
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is equipped with the
p
-Wasserstein distance

\mathcal{W}_{p}(\mu,\nu)=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x-y|^{p}\pi(dx,dy)\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}},\quad\text{for $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$},

where
\Pi(\mu,\nu)
is the set of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that for any
\pi\in\Pi(\mu,v)
,
\pi(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\cdot)=\mu
and
\pi(\cdot\times\mathbb{R}^{d})=\nu
. We call
(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathcal{W}_{p})
the
p
-Wasserstein space over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and it is a Polish space. Finally, we denote by
\operatorname{Supp}(\mu)
the support of
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. For a map
f:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
, we adopt the notion of
L
-derivative (see
[
12
]
for instance) which is recalled as follows:

Definition 2.1
.

The function
f:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is said to be first order
L
-differentiable if its lifting
F:[0,T]\times L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
;
F(t,\xi):=f(t,\mathcal{L}(\xi))
admits a continuous Fréchet derivative
D_{\xi}F:[0,T]\times L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Remark 2.2
.

By
[
14
, Proposition 5.25]
, if
f
is first order
L
-differentiable, then it may be shown that there is a measurable function, denoted by
\partial_{\mu}f(t,\mu)(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, such that
D_{\xi}F(t,\xi)=\partial_{\mu}f(t,\mu)(\xi)
for any
(t,\mu)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\xi\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\mathcal{L}(\xi)=\mu
. We say that
\partial_{\mu}f:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the first order
L
-derivative of
f
.

Definition 2.2
.

The function
f:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is said to be second order
L
-differentiable if
f
is first order
L
-differentiable and for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the function
\mu\mapsto\partial_{\mu}f(t,\mu)(x)
is
L
-differentiable, i.e., the lifting
F^{\prime}:L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
of
\mu\mapsto\partial_{\mu}f(t,\mu)(x)
admits a continuous Fréchet derivative
D_{\xi}F^{\prime}:[0,T]\times L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
.

Remark 2.3
.

Similar to Remark
2.2
, if
f
is second order
L
-differentiable, then there is a measurable function, denoted by
\partial_{\mu}^{2}f(t,\mu)(x,\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, such that
D_{\xi}F^{\prime}(t,\xi^{\prime})=\partial_{\mu}^{2}f(t,\mu)(x,\xi^{\prime})
for any
(t,\mu,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\xi^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\mathcal{L}(\xi^{\prime})=\mu
. We say that
\partial^{2}_{\mu}f:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is the second order
L
-derivative of
f
.

As in
[
5
]
, we define the set of fully second order
L
-differentiable functions and the set of partially second order
L
-differentiable functions.

Definition 2.3
.

The set
C_{1}^{1,2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
consists of all continuous functions
f:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying the following:

1.

the derivatives
\partial_{t}f(t,\mu)
,
\partial_{\mu}f(t,\mu)(x)
,
\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}f(t,\mu)(x)
,
\partial_{\mu}^{2}f(t,\mu)(x,x^{\prime})
exist and are jointly continuous in the respective arguments;

2.

there is a constant
C_{f}\geq 0
such that for any
(t,\mu,x,x^{\prime})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle|\partial_{\mu}f(t,\mu)(x)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{f}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big($}}}}}1+|x|\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big)$}}}}};

\displaystyle|\partial_{t}f(t,\mu)|+|\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}f(t,\mu)(x)|+\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{291272}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big|$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{291272}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big|$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{291272}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big|$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{291272}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big|$}}}}}\partial_{\mu}^{2}f(t,\mu)(x,x^{\prime})\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{291272}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big|$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{291272}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big|$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{291272}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big|$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{291272}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big|$}}}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{f}.

We define the operator
\mathcal{H}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}u(\mu):=\frac{d^{2}}{dw^{2}}u((I_{d}+w)_{\sharp}\mu)\Big|_{w=0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},

where
(I_{d}+w)_{\sharp}\mu
is the pushforward measure associated with map
I_{d}+w:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined by
x\mapsto x+w
for
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
; and we are differentiating with respect to finite-dimensional
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Definition 2.4
.

The set
{PC}_{1}^{1,2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
consists of all continuous functions
f:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying the following:

1.

the derivatives
\partial_{t}f(t,\mu)
,
\partial_{\mu}f(t,\mu)(x)
,
\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}f(t,\mu)(x)
,
\mathcal{H}f(\mu)
exist and are jointly continuous in the respective arguments;

2.

there exists
C_{f}\geq 0
such that for any
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(t,\mu)}\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it holds that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\partial_{\mu}f(t,\mu)(x)\right|^{2}\mu(dx)

\displaystyle\leq C_{f}\left(1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{2}\mu(dx)\right);

\displaystyle|\partial_{t}f(t,\mu)|+|\mathcal{H}f(t,\mu)|+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}f(t,\mu)(x)\right|^{2}\mu(dx)

\displaystyle\leq C_{f}.

We have the following lemma relating
PC_{1}^{1,2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
C_{1}^{1,2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

Lemma 2.5
.

If
u\in C_{1}^{1,2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, then we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}u(t,\mu)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}u(t,\mu)(x)\mu(dx)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial^{2}_{\mu}u(t,\mu)(x,\tilde{x})\mu(dx)\mu(d\tilde{x}),

and hence
u\in PC_{1}^{1,2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

Proof.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
X\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\mathcal{L}(X)=\mu
. We denote the lifting of
u\in C_{1}^{1,2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
to Hilbert space by
u^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dw}u^{*}(t,X+w)=\mathbb{E}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big[$}}}}}\partial_{\mu}u(t,\mathcal{L}(X+w))(X+w)\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big]$}}}}}.

Besides, we consider

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dw}\partial_{\mu}u(t,\mathcal{L}(X+w))(X+w)

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}u(t,\mathcal{L}(X+w))(X+w)+\bar{\mathbb{E}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big[$}}}}}\partial_{\mu}^{2}u(t,\mathcal{L}(X+w))(X+w,\bar{X}+w)\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big]$}}}}},

where
\bar{X}
is an independent copy of
X
and
\bar{\mathbb{E}}
is the expectation with respect to
\bar{X}
. We assume that
|w|\leq 1
and use the fact that
u\in C_{1}^{1,2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
to yield that

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{d}{dw}\partial_{\mu}u(t,\mathcal{L}(X+w))(X+w)\bigg|\leq C_{u},

for some constant
C_{u}>0
independent of
w
and
X
. By the dominated convergence theorem we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dw}\frac{d}{dw}u^{*}(t,X+w)\Big|_{w=0}=\,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dw}\mathbb{E}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big[$}}}}}\partial_{\mu}u(t,\mathcal{L}(X+w))(X+w)\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big]$}}}}}\Big|_{w=0}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}u(t,\mathcal{L}(X+w))(X+w)+\bar{\mathbb{E}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big[$}}}}}\partial_{\mu}^{2}u(t,\mathcal{L}(X+w))(X+w,\bar{X}+w)\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big]$}}}}}\Big\}\bigg|_{w=0}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}u(t,\mu)(x)\mu(dx)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial^{2}_{\mu}u(t,\mu)(x,\tilde{x})\mu(dx)\mu(d\tilde{x}).

∎

We have the following extension of Itô formula for functions in
PC_{1}^{1,2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. This is inspired by
[
5
]
.

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
b_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\sigma_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be two
\mathbb{F}
-adapted processes such that there exists
L>0
satisfying
|b_{t}|\vee|\sigma_{t}|\leq L

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for all
t\in[0,T]
. Let
\sigma_{t}^{0}:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be deterministic and
\xi\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
. Consider the following
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued Itô process:

\displaystyle X_{t}=\xi+\int^{t}_{0}b_{s}ds+\int^{t}_{0}\sigma_{s}dW_{s}+\int^{t}_{0}\sigma_{s}^{0}dW_{s}^{0},\quad\text{for }t\in[0,T].

We write
\mu_{t}^{W^{0}}
for
\mathbb{P}_{X_{t}}^{W^{0}}
, then it holds
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s. that for
\varphi\in PC_{1}^{1,2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,

\displaystyle\varphi(t,\mu_{t}^{W^{0}})=\,

\displaystyle\varphi(0,\mu_{0}^{W^{0}})+\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{t}\varphi(s,\mu_{s}^{W^{0}})ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}^{1}\big[\partial_{\mu}\varphi(s,\mu_{s}^{W^{0}})(X_{s})\cdot b_{s}\big]ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}^{1}\big[\sigma_{s}^{0;\top}\partial_{\mu}\varphi(s,\mu_{s}^{W^{0}})(X_{s})\big]\cdot dW_{s}^{0}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}^{1}\Big\{\operatorname{tr}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big[$}}}}}\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}\varphi(s,\mu_{s}^{W^{0}})(X_{s})\sigma_{s}\sigma_{s}^{\top}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big]$}}}}}\Big\}ds

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\operatorname{tr}\Big[\mathcal{H}\varphi(s,\mu_{s}^{W^{0}})\sigma_{s}^{0}\sigma_{s}^{0;\top}\Big]ds.

Proof.

For
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s.
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
, we consider

\displaystyle X_{t}(\omega^{0},\cdot)=\xi(\omega^{0},\cdot)+\left(\int_{0}^{t}b_{r}dr\right)(\omega^{0},\cdot)+\left(\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{r}dW_{r}\right)(\omega^{0},\cdot)+\left(\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{r}^{0}dW_{r}^{0}\right)(\omega^{0}).

We define
Y_{t}^{\omega^{0}}(\cdot):=X_{t}(\omega^{0},\cdot)-\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big($}}}}}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{r}^{0}dW_{r}^{0}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big)$}}}}}(\omega^{0})
. Applying the standard mean field Itô formula (see
[
14
, Proposition 5.102]
) to
\varphi(t,\mathcal{L}(Y^{\omega^{0}}_{t}+y))
for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\varphi\in PC_{1}^{1,2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, we conclude that for
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s.
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
,

\displaystyle\varphi(t,\mathcal{L}(Y_{t}^{\omega^{0}}+y))=\,

\displaystyle\varphi(0,\mathcal{L}(Y_{0}^{\omega^{0}}+y))+\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{t}\varphi(s,\mathcal{L}(Y_{s}^{\omega^{0}}+y))ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}^{1}\Big[\partial_{\mu}\varphi(s,\mathcal{L}(Y_{s}^{\omega^{0}}+y))(Y_{s}^{\omega^{0}}+y)\cdot b_{s}\Big]ds

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}^{1}\Big\{\operatorname{tr}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big[$}}}}}\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}\varphi(s,\mathcal{L}(Y_{s}^{\omega^{0}}+y))(Y_{s}^{\omega^{0}}+y)\sigma_{s}\sigma_{s}^{\top}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big]$}}}}}\Big\}ds.

We define
\Psi(s,y):=\varphi(s,\mathcal{L}(Y_{s}^{\omega^{0}}+y))
. By the finite-dimensional Itô-Kunita-Wentzell formula
[
24
, Theorem 2.3]
, we have

\displaystyle\hskip-10.0pt\Psi\left(t,\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{0}_{s}dW_{s}^{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\Psi(0,0)+\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{t}\varphi\Big(s,\mathcal{L}\Big(Y_{s}^{\omega^{0}}+\int_{0}^{s}\sigma^{0}_{r}dW_{r}^{0}\Big)\Big)ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}^{1}\Big[\partial_{\mu}\varphi\Big(s,\mathcal{L}\Big(Y_{s}^{\omega^{0}}+\int_{0}^{s}\sigma^{0}_{r}dW_{r}^{0}\Big)\Big)\Big(Y_{s}^{\omega^{0}}+\int_{0}^{s}\sigma^{0}_{r}dW_{r}^{0}\Big)\cdot b_{s}\Big]ds

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}^{1}\Big[\operatorname{tr}\Big\{\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}\varphi\Big(s,\mathcal{L}\Big(Y_{s}^{\omega^{0}}+\int_{0}^{s}\sigma^{0}_{r}dW_{r}^{0}\Big)\Big)\Big(Y_{s}^{\omega^{0}}+\int_{0}^{s}\sigma^{0}_{r}dW_{r}^{0}\Big)\sigma_{s}\sigma_{s}^{\top}\Big\}\Big]ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}^{1}\Big[\sigma_{s}^{0;\top}\partial_{\mu}\varphi\Big(s,\mathcal{L}\Big(Y_{s}^{\omega^{0}}+\int_{0}^{s}\sigma^{0}_{r}dW_{r}^{0}\Big)\Big)\Big(Y_{s}^{\omega^{0}}+\int_{0}^{s}\sigma^{0}_{r}dW_{r}^{0}\Big)\Big]\cdot dW_{s}^{0}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\Big[\operatorname{tr}\Big\{\partial_{y}^{2}\varphi\Big(s,\mathcal{L}\Big(Y_{s}^{\omega^{0}}+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{0}_{s}dW_{s}^{0}\Big)\Big)\sigma_{s}^{0}\sigma_{s}^{0;\top}\Big\}\Big]ds

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\varphi(0,\mu_{0}^{W^{0}})+\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{t}\varphi(s,\mu_{s}^{W^{0}})ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}^{1}\big[\partial_{\mu}\varphi(s,\mu_{s}^{W^{0}})(X_{s})\cdot b_{s}\big]ds

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}^{1}\big[\operatorname{tr}\{\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}\varphi(s,\mu_{s}^{W^{0}})(X_{s})\sigma_{s}\sigma_{s}^{\top}\}\big]ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}^{1}\big[\sigma_{s}^{0;\top}\partial_{\mu}\varphi(s,\mu_{s}^{W^{0}})(X_{s})\big]\cdot dW_{s}^{0}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\Big[\operatorname{tr}\Big\{\mathcal{H}\varphi(s,\mu_{s}^{W^{0}})\sigma_{s}^{0}\sigma_{s}^{0;\top}\Big\}\Big]ds.

∎

2.3
Assumptions and the Control Problem

Define the functions:

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times A\to\mathbb{R}^{d},

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times A\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},

\displaystyle\sigma^{0}

\displaystyle:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times A\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}.

Throughout this work, we use the following assumptions:

Assumption
.

(A).
The functions
b
,
\sigma
,
\sigma^{0}
,
f
and
g
satisfy the following:

1.

the functions
b
,
\sigma
,
\sigma^{0}
,
f
and
g
are continuous;

2.

there exist constants
K\geq 0
and
\beta\in(0,1]
such that for any
a\in A
,
(t,x,\mu)
,
(t^{\prime},x^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it holds that

\displaystyle\big|b(t,x,\mu,a)-b(t^{\prime},x^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},a)\big|+\big|\sigma(t,x,a)-\sigma(t^{\prime},x^{\prime},a)\big|+\big|\sigma^{0}(t)-\sigma^{0}(t^{\prime})\big|

\displaystyle+\big|f(t,x,\mu,a)-f(t^{\prime},x^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},a)\big|+\big|g(x,\mu)-g(x^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\big|\leq K\big[|x-x^{\prime}|+|t-t^{\prime}|^{\beta}+\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\big],

\displaystyle\big|b(t,x,\mu,a)\big|+\big|\sigma(t,x,a)\big|+\big|\sigma^{0}(t)\big|+\big|f(t,x,\mu,a)\big|+\big|g(x,\mu)\big|\leq K.

Assumption
.

(B).
For any
a\in A
, the function
\sigma(\cdot,\cdot,a)
belongs to
C^{1,2}\left([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
. Moreover, there exists a constant
K\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{t}\sigma(t,x,a)\right|+\left|\partial_{x_{i}}\sigma(t,x,a)\right|+\big|\partial_{x_{i}x_{j}}^{2}\sigma(t,x,a)\big|+\left|\partial_{t}\sigma^{0}(t)\right|\leq K,

for all
(t,x,a)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times A
and any
i,j=1,2,\ldots,d
.

Assumption
.

(A*).

Suppose that Assumption (A) holds and we assume further that there exist constants
K\geq 0
and
\beta\in(0,1]
such that for any
a\in A
,
(t,x,\mu)
,
(t^{\prime},x^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it holds that

\displaystyle\big|b(t,x,\mu,a)-b(t^{\prime},x^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},a)\big|+\big|f(t,x,\mu,a)-f(t^{\prime},x^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},a)\big|+\big|g(x,\mu)-g(x^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\big|

\displaystyle\leq K\big[|x-x^{\prime}|+|t-t^{\prime}|^{\beta}+\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\big].

Remark 2.4
.

Assumption (A*) is a strengthening assumption of Assumption (A). We write Assumption (A) and Assumption (A*) separately to emphasise that some of the lemmas and theorems still hold without assuming the stronger condition in Assumption (A*).

Remark 2.5
.

Here, the coefficients
b
,
\sigma
,
f
and
g
are assumed to be
\mathcal{W}_{1}
-Lipschitz continuous in Assumption (A*), which is different from the
\mathcal{W}_{2}
-Lipschitz continuity assumption in
[
17
, Assumption (A)]
. The main reason of adopting
\mathcal{W}_{1}
-Lipschitz continuity instead of
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is to provide a better estimate when we establish the smooth approximation of the coefficients in Lemma
5.2
. We note that the inequalities
[
17
, (A.6) and (A.7)]
are invalid if only
\mathcal{W}_{2}
-Lipschitz continuity is assumed as in
[
17
, Assumption (A)]
. This leads to flaws in the proofs of
[
17
, Theorem 5.1, Theorem A.7]
. See more discussions in Remark
5.1
.

For every
t\in[0,T]
,
\xi\in L^{2}(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}_{t}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t}
, we consider the solution
X^{t,\xi,\alpha}
of the following state dynamics:

\displaystyle X_{s}

\displaystyle=\xi+\int_{t}^{s}b(r,X_{r},\mathbb{P}_{X_{r}}^{W^{0}},\alpha_{r})dr+\int_{t}^{s}\sigma(r,X_{r},\alpha_{r})dW_{r}+\int_{t}^{s}\sigma^{0}(r)dW^{0}_{r},\hskip 5.0pt\text{ for $s\in[t,T]$,}

(2.3)

where
\mathbb{P}_{X_{r}}^{W^{0}}
denotes the conditional distribution of
X_{r}
given
W^{0}
. We are subject to the cost functional:

\displaystyle J(t,\xi,\alpha):=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{T}f\Big(s,X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}^{W^{0}}_{X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha}},\alpha_{s}\Big)ds+g\Big(X_{T}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}_{X_{T}^{t,\xi,\alpha}}^{W^{0}}\Big)\Bigg].

(2.4)

Finally, we define the value function
V
to be

\displaystyle V(t,\xi):=\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t}}J(t,\xi,\alpha),\quad\text{for any }(t,\xi)\in[0,T]\times L^{2}(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}_{t}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(2.5)

3
Basic Properties

This section collects some basic properties. First we give some standard results concerning the regularity of the solution of the SDE in (
2.3
) and the value function
V
. Proofs are standard and therefore omitted, and readers are referred to
[
45
]
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Suppose that Assumption (A) holds. For every
t\in[0,T]
,
\xi\in L^{2}(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1}_{t},\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t}
, there exists a unique (up to
\mathbb{P}
-indistinguishability) continuous
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable solution
X^{t,\xi,\alpha}=(X^{t,\xi,\alpha}_{s})_{s\in[t,T]}
of equation (
2.3
). Moreover, for any
p\geq 2
, there is a constant
C
depending only on
p
,
K
,
T
,
d
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{s\in[t,T]}|X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha}|^{p}\Big]^{1/p}\leq C\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big($}}}}}1+\mathbb{E}|\xi|^{p}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big)$}}}}}}^{1/p};

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{s\in[t,T]}|X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha}-X_{s}^{t,\xi^{\prime},\alpha}|^{p}\Big]^{1/p}\leq C\mathbb{E}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big($}}}}}|\xi-\xi^{\prime}|^{p}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big)$}}}}}}^{1/p};

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{s\in[t,t+h]}|X^{t,\xi,\alpha}_{s}-\xi|^{2}\Big]\leq Ch,

(3.3)

for any
t\in[0,T]
,
h\in[0,T-t]
,
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1}_{t},\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose that Assumption
(A)
holds. The function
V
satisfies the following properties:

1.

V
is bounded and jointly continuous;

2.

there exists a constant
C\geq 0
such that for any
t,t^{\prime}\in[0,T]
,
\xi
,
\xi^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{t},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle|V(t,\xi)-V(t^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})|\leq C\Big[\mathbb{E}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big($}}}}}|\xi-\xi^{\prime}|^{2}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big)$}}}}}}^{1/2}+|t-t^{\prime}|^{1/2}\Big].

The constant
C
depends on
d
,
K
,
T
and independent of
t
,
t^{\prime}
,
\xi
,
\xi^{\prime}
,
\alpha
.

Proposition 3.3

(Law Invariance)
.

Suppose that Assumption (A) holds. For every
t\in[0,T]
and
\xi
,
\eta\in L^{2}({\Omega}^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1}_{t},\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with
\mathcal{L}(\xi)=\mathcal{L}(\eta)
, it holds that

V(t,\xi)=V(t,\eta)
.

Proof.

This proof is modified from
[
18
, Theorem 3.6]
to suit the case with common noise. We only highlight the differences, and interested readers are referred to
[
18
, Theorem 3.6]
. Fix
t\in[0,T]
. Let
\xi,\eta\in L^{2}(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1}_{t},\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\mathcal{L}(\xi)=\mathcal{L}(\eta)
. By Proposition
3.2
, the function
\xi\mapsto V(t,\xi)
is continuous. Thus, without loss of generality (see
[
18
, Theorem 3.6, Substep 2.2]
), we consider the case where
\xi
,
\eta
are discrete, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\xi)=\mathcal{L}(\eta)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\delta_{x^{i}},

for some
\{x^{1},x^{2},\ldots,x^{m}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
x^{i}
being distinct. Here
\delta_{x^{i}}
is the Dirac measure at
x^{i}
. For
i=1,2,\ldots,m
, the numbers
p_{i}
satisfy
p_{i}>0
and
\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}=1
. By
[
18
, Lemma B.3]
, there exist two
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{t}
-measurable random variables
U_{\xi}
and
U_{\eta}
with uniform distribution on
[0,1]
, such that
\xi
and
U_{\xi}
are independent, as are
\eta
and
U_{\eta}
. Let
\mathbb{F}^{W,W^{0},t}
be the
\mathbb{P}
-completion of the filtration generated by
(W_{s\vee t}-W_{t})_{s\geq 0}
and
(W^{0}_{s})_{s\geq 0}
and let
Prog(\mathbb{F}^{W,W^{0},t})
be the
\sigma
-algebra of
[0,T]\times\Omega
of all
\mathbb{F}^{W,W^{0},t}
-progressively measurable sets. Fixing
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t}
, we claim that there exists a measurable function:

\displaystyle a:\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big($}}}}}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1],Prog(\mathbb{F}^{W,W^{0},t})\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathcal{B}([0,1])\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big)$}}}}}\to(A,\mathcal{B}(A))

such that

\displaystyle\hskip-10.0pt\mathcal{L}\Big(\xi,(a_{s}(\xi,U_{\xi}))_{s\in[t,T]},(W_{s}^{0})_{s\geq 0},(W_{s}-W_{t})_{s\in[t,T]}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big(\xi,(\alpha_{s})_{s\in[t,T]},(W_{s}^{0})_{s\geq 0},(W_{s}-W_{t})_{s\in[t,T]}\Big)\,.

(3.4)

This claim may be seen from the following: denote

\displaystyle\hat{\Omega}

\displaystyle=[0,T]\times\Omega,

\displaystyle\quad\hat{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}([0,T])\otimes\mathcal{F},

\displaystyle\quad\hat{\mathbb{P}}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{T}\otimes\mathbb{P},

\displaystyle\quad\bar{E}

\displaystyle=[0,T]\times\Omega,

\displaystyle\quad\bar{\mathscr{E}}

\displaystyle=Prog(\mathbb{F}^{W,W^{0},t}),

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=\bar{E}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},

\displaystyle\quad\mathscr{E}

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathscr{E}}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

with
\lambda_{T}
being the uniform distribution on
([0,T],\mathcal{B}([0,T]))
. Consider the canonical extension of
U_{\xi}
to
\hat{\Omega}
, denoted by
\hat{U}_{\xi}
. Define
\mathcal{I}^{W,W^{0},t}:(\hat{\Omega},\hat{\mathcal{F}})\to(\bar{E},\bar{\mathscr{E}})
the identity map and
\Gamma:=(\mathcal{I}^{W,W^{0},t},\xi)
. Then
\Gamma:(\hat{\Omega},\hat{\mathcal{F}},\hat{\mathbb{P}})\to(E,\mathscr{E})
is independent of
U_{\xi}
as
U_{\xi}
is
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{t}
measurable. Applying
[
30
, Theorem 6.10]
, we get a map
\bar{a}:[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1]\to(A,\mathcal{B}(A))
, being measurable with respect to the
\sigma
-algebra
Prog(\mathbb{F}^{W,W^{0},t})\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathcal{B}([0,1])
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\Gamma,\bar{a}(\Gamma,\hat{U}_{\xi}))=\mathcal{L}(\Gamma,\alpha).

From the definition of
\Gamma
we conclude that

\displaystyle\hskip-10.0pt\mathcal{L}\Big(\xi,(\bar{a}_{s}(\xi,U_{\xi}))_{s\in[0,T]},(W_{s}-W_{t})_{s\in[t,T]},(W^{0}_{s})_{s\geq 0}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big(\xi,(\alpha_{s})_{s\in[0,T]},(W_{s}-W_{t})_{s\in[t,T]},(W^{0}_{s})_{s\geq 0}\Big).

Given an arbitrary
u_{0}\in A
, we can choose the required measurable function
a:[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1]\to A
to be

\displaystyle a_{s}(\omega,x,u):=u_{0}\mathbbm{1}_{[0,t)}(s)+\bar{a}_{s}(\omega,x,u)\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]}(s),\quad\text{for any }(s,\omega,x,u)\in[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times A.

Then
(a_{s}(\xi,U_{\xi}))_{s\in[0,T]}
is
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable and (
3
) is satisfied. The measurable function
a
satisfies

\displaystyle\hskip-10.0pt\mathcal{L}\Big(\xi,(\alpha_{s})_{s\in[t,T]},(W_{s}-W_{t})_{s\in[t,T]},(W^{0}_{s})_{s\geq 0}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big(\xi,(a_{s}(\xi,U_{\xi}))_{s\in[t,T]},(W_{s}-W_{t})_{s\in[t,T]},(W^{0}_{s})_{s\geq 0}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big(\eta,(a_{s}(\eta,U_{\eta}))_{s\in[t,T]},(W_{s}-W_{t})_{s\in[t,T]},(W^{0}_{s})_{s\geq 0}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big(\eta,(\beta_{s})_{s\in[t,T]},(W_{s}-W_{t})_{s\in[t,T]},(W^{0}_{s})_{s\geq 0}\Big),

where
\beta_{s}:=a_{s}(\eta,U_{\eta})
. It can be shown therefore that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big($}}}}}(X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha})_{s\in[t,T]},(\alpha_{s})_{s\in[t,T]},(W^{0}_{s})_{s\geq 0}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big)$}}}}}=\mathcal{L}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big($}}}}}(X_{s}^{t,\eta,\beta})_{s\in[t,T]},(\beta_{s})_{s\in[t,T]},(W^{0}_{s})_{s\geq 0}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big)$}}}}},

and therefore
J(t,\xi,\alpha)=J(t,\eta,\beta)
, implying
V(t,\xi)=V(t,\eta)
.
∎

Theorem 3.4

(Dynamic Programming Principle)
.

Suppose that Assumption (A) holds. The value function
V
satisfies the dynamic programming principle: for every
t\in[0,T]
and
\xi\in L^{2}(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}_{t}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it holds that

\displaystyle V(t,\xi)=

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t}}\Bigg\{\inf_{s\in[t,T]}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{s}f\Big(r,X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}^{W^{0}}_{X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha}},\alpha_{r}\Big)dr+V(s,X^{t,\xi,\alpha}_{s})\Bigg]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t}}\Bigg\{\sup_{s\in[t,T]}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{s}f\Big(r,X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}^{W^{0}}_{X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha}},\alpha_{r}\Big)dr+V(s,X^{t,\xi,\alpha}_{s})\Bigg]\Bigg\}.

Proof.

Put

\displaystyle\underline{\Lambda}(t,\xi):=\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t}}\Bigg\{\inf_{s\in[t,T]}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{s}f\Big(r,X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}_{X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha}}^{W^{0}},\alpha_{r}\Big)dr+V(s,X^{t,\xi,\alpha}_{s})\Bigg]\Bigg\},

\displaystyle\overline{\Lambda}(t,\xi):=\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t}}\Bigg\{\sup_{s\in[t,T]}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{s}f\Big(r,X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}_{X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha}}^{W^{0}},\alpha_{r}\Big)dr+V(s,X^{t,\xi,\alpha}_{s})\Bigg]\Bigg\}.

We shall show
\overline{\Lambda}(t,\xi)\leq V(t,\xi)\leq\underline{\Lambda}(t,\xi)
. We start with
V(t,\xi)\leq\underline{\Lambda}(t,\xi)
.
Let
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t}
be arbitrary. Note that our value function
V(t,\xi)
is in fact defined only for
\xi\in L^{2}(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}_{t}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, thus
V(s,X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha})
is in fact random in the sense that
V(s,X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha})(\omega^{0})=V(s,X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha}(\omega^{0},\cdot))
. Note that by the uniqueness of the SDE, for
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s.
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
, the process
\big(X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha}(\omega^{0},\cdot)\big)_{r\in[s,T]}
is the unique solution of

\displaystyle X_{r}(\omega^{0},\cdot)=

\displaystyle\Big(X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha}+\int_{s}^{r}b(k,X_{k},\mathbb{P}_{X_{k}}^{W^{0}},\alpha_{k})dk+\int_{s}^{r}\sigma(k,X_{k},\alpha_{k})dW_{k}+\int_{s}^{r}\sigma^{0}(k)dW^{0}_{k}\Big)(\omega^{0},\cdot),

thus for
\mathbb{P}^{0}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s.
(\omega^{0},\omega^{00})\in\Omega^{0}\times\Omega^{0}
,
\big(X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha}(\omega^{0}\otimes_{s}\omega^{00},\cdot)\big)_{r\in[s,T]}
solves

\displaystyle X_{r}(\omega^{0}\otimes_{s}\omega^{00},\cdot)=\,

\displaystyle\Big(X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha}+\int_{s}^{r}b(k,X_{k},\mathbb{P}_{X_{k}}^{W^{0}},\alpha_{k})dk+\int_{s}^{r}\sigma(k,X_{k},\alpha_{k})dW_{k}

\displaystyle+\int_{s}^{r}\sigma^{0}(k)dW^{0}_{k}\Big)(\omega^{0}\otimes_{s}\omega^{00},\cdot).

(3.5)

Since
X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha}(\omega^{0},\cdot)
is
\mathcal{F}_{s}^{t}
-measurable, we have
X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha}(\omega^{0}\otimes_{s}\omega^{00},\cdot)=X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha}(\omega^{0},\cdot)
and for
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s.
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
,
(\ref{dy_1})
is equivalent to

\displaystyle X_{r}(\omega^{0}\otimes_{s}\omega^{00},\cdot)

\displaystyle=X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha}(\omega^{0},\cdot)+\Big(\int_{s}^{r}b(k,X_{k},\mathbb{P}_{X_{k}}^{W^{0}},\alpha_{k})dk+\int_{s}^{r}\sigma(k,X_{k},\alpha_{k})dW_{k}+\int_{s}^{r}\sigma^{0}(k)dW^{0}_{k}\Big)(\omega^{0}\otimes_{s}\omega^{00},\cdot).

For
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s.
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
and
\tilde{\omega}=(\tilde{\omega}^{0},\tilde{\omega}^{1})\in\Omega^{0}\times\Omega^{1}
, we define
\hat{\alpha}^{\omega^{0}}_{r}(\tilde{\omega}):=\alpha_{r}(\omega^{0}\otimes_{s}\tilde{\omega}^{0},\tilde{\omega}^{1})
. Then for
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s.
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
,
X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha}(\omega^{0}\otimes_{s}\omega^{00},\cdot)
solves

\displaystyle Y_{r}(\omega^{00},\omega^{1})=\,

\displaystyle X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha}(\omega^{0},\omega^{1})

\displaystyle+\Big(\int_{s}^{r}b(k,Y_{k},\mathbb{P}_{Y_{k}}^{W^{0}},\hat{\alpha}^{\omega^{0}}_{k})dk+\int_{s}^{r}\sigma(k,Y_{k},\hat{\alpha}^{\omega^{0}}_{k})dW_{k}+\int_{s}^{r}\sigma^{0}(k)dW^{0}_{k}\Big)(\omega^{00},\omega^{1}).

Therefore

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}V(s,X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha})=\,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{0}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big[$}}}}}{V(s,X_{s}^{t,\xi,\alpha}(\omega^{0},\cdot))}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big]$}}}}}

\displaystyle\geq\,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{0}\Bigg\{\mathbb{E}^{00}\mathbb{E}^{1}\Bigg[\int_{s}^{T}f\left(r,X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha}(\omega^{0}\otimes_{s}\omega^{00},\omega^{1}),\mathbb{P}^{W^{0}}_{X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha}}(\omega^{0}\otimes_{s}\omega^{00}),\hat{\alpha}^{\omega^{0}}_{r}(\omega^{00},\omega^{1})\right)dr

\displaystyle\hskip 60.0pt+g\left(X_{T}^{t,\xi,\alpha}(\omega^{0}\otimes_{s}\omega^{00},\omega^{1}),\mathbb{P}_{X_{T}^{t,\xi,\alpha}}^{W^{0}}(\omega^{0}\otimes_{s}\omega^{00})\right)\Bigg]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{s}^{T}f(r,X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}^{W^{0}}_{X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha}},\alpha_{r})dr+g\Big(X_{T}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}_{X_{T}^{t,\xi,\alpha}}^{W^{0}}\Big)\Bigg].

Therefore

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{s}f\Big(r,X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}_{X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha}}^{W^{0}},\alpha_{r}\Big)dr+V(s,X^{t,\xi,\alpha}_{s})\Bigg]\geq J(t,\xi,\alpha),

since
s\in[t,T]
is arbitrary, we have

\displaystyle\inf_{s\in[t,T]}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{s}f\Big(r,X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}_{X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha}}^{W^{0}},\alpha_{r}\Big)dr+V(s,X^{t,\xi,\alpha}_{s})\Bigg]\geq J(t,\xi,\alpha).

As
\alpha
is arbitrary, so

\displaystyle\underline{\Lambda}(t,\xi)\geq V(t,\xi).

On the other hand, for any
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{t}
,
s\in[t,T]
, by the continuity of
J(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
and
V(\cdot,\cdot)
, we use the measurable selection theorem (see
[
43
]
for instance) to find a
\gamma:(\Omega^{0},\mathcal{F}_{s}^{W^{0}},\mathbb{P}^{0})\to(\mathcal{A}_{s},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}_{s}})
such that

\displaystyle V(s,X^{t,\xi,\beta}_{s}(\omega^{0}))\leq\,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{s}^{T}f\left(r,X_{r}^{s,X^{t,\xi,\beta}_{s}(\omega^{0}),\gamma(\omega^{0})},\mathbb{P}^{W^{0}}_{X_{r}^{s,X^{t,\xi,\beta}_{s}(\omega^{0}),\gamma(\omega^{0})}},\gamma_{r}(\omega^{0})\right)dr

\displaystyle\hskip 25.0pt+g\left(X_{T}^{s,X^{t,\xi,\beta}_{s}(\omega^{0}),\gamma(\omega^{0})},\mathbb{P}_{X_{T}^{s,X^{t,\xi,\beta}_{s}(\omega^{0}),\gamma(\omega^{0})}}^{W^{0}}\right)\Bigg]+\varepsilon.

Put
\Gamma_{r}(\omega^{0},\omega^{1}):=\beta_{r}(\omega^{0},\omega^{1})\mathbbm{1}_{[0,s)}(r)+\gamma_{r}(\omega^{0})(\omega^{0},\omega^{1})\mathbbm{1}_{[s,T]}(r)
. Then by
[
39
, Lemma 2.1]
, we have
\Gamma\in\mathcal{A}_{t}
and

\displaystyle\hskip-10.0pt\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{s}f(r,X_{r}^{t,\xi,\beta},\mathbb{P}_{X_{r}^{t,\xi,\beta}}^{W^{0}},\beta_{r})dr+V(s,X^{t,\xi,\beta}_{s})\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{T}f(r,X_{r}^{t,\xi,\Gamma},\mathbb{P}_{X_{r}^{t,\xi,\Gamma}}^{W^{0}},\Gamma_{r})dr+g(X_{T}^{t,\xi,\Gamma},\mathbb{P}_{X_{T}^{t,\xi,\Gamma}}^{W^{0}})\Bigg]+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle V(t,\xi)+\varepsilon.

Taking supremum in
s\in[t,T]
on both sides gives

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[t,T]}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{s}f(r,X_{r}^{t,\xi,\beta},\mathbb{P}_{X_{r}^{t,\xi,\beta}}^{W^{0}},\beta_{r})dr+V(s,X^{t,\xi,\beta}_{s})\Bigg]\leq V(t,\xi)+\varepsilon

and by the arbitrariness of
\varepsilon>0
and
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{t}
, we have
\overline{\Lambda}(t,\xi)\leq V(t,\xi)
.
∎

From Proposition
3.3
, we can define a function
v(t,\mu):[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
such that for any
t\in[0,T]
and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it is given by

\displaystyle v(t,\mu):=V(t,\xi)

(3.6)

for any
\xi\in L^{2}(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}_{t}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\mathcal{L}(\xi)=\mu
, where
V(t,\xi)
is defined in (
2.5
). From Theorem
3.4
and (
3.6
), the dynamic programming principle may therefore be recast as

Theorem 3.5

(Dynamic Programming Principle)
.

Suppose that Assumption (A) hold. The value function
v
satisfies the dynamic programming principle: for every
t\in[0,T]
and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it holds that

\displaystyle v(t,\mu)=

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t}}\sup_{s\in[t,T]}\Bigg\{\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{s}f\Big(r,X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}^{W^{0}}_{X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha}},\alpha_{r}\Big)dr+v\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big($}}}}}s,\mathbb{P}_{X^{t,\xi,\alpha}_{s}}^{W^{0}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big)$}}}}}\Bigg]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t}}\inf_{s\in[t,T]}\Bigg\{\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{s}f\Big(r,X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha},\mathbb{P}^{W^{0}}_{X_{r}^{t,\xi,\alpha}},\alpha_{r}\Big)dr+v\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big($}}}}}s,\mathbb{P}_{X^{t,\xi,\alpha}_{s}}^{W^{0}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big)$}}}}}\Bigg]\Bigg\},

for any
\xi\in L^{2}(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}_{t}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\mathcal{L}(\xi)=\mu
.

4
Smooth Variational Principle

This section is devoted to the study of the regularity of our chosen gauge function for the comparison theorem of viscosity solutions. As outlined in
[
17
]
, the key to the comparison theorem of viscosity subsolutions/supersolutions is the existence of a smooth gauge function, so that when the value function is slightly perturbed by the gauge function, it can attain its maximum/minimum, as indicated by the Borwein-Preiss variational principle. To this purpose, we shall make use of the Gaussian regularized sliced Wasserstein distance defined in
[
4
, Section 2]
as our gauge function. By a gauge function, we mean the following:

Definition 4.1
.

Let
d_{2}
be a metric on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),d_{2})
is complete. Consider the set
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
endowed with the metric
((t,\mu),(s,\nu))\mapsto|t-s|+d_{2}(\mu,\nu)
. A map
\rho:([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{2}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
is said to be a gauge-type function if the following hold:

1.

\rho((t,\mu),(t,\mu))=0
, for every
(t,\mu)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
;

2.

\rho
is continuous on
([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{2}
;

3.

for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta>0
such that, for any
(t,\mu),(s,\nu)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the inequality
\rho((t,\mu),(s,\nu))\leq\eta
implies
|t-s|+d_{2}(\mu,\nu)\leq\varepsilon
.

4.1
Properties of the Smooth Gauge Function

We recall the definition of the Gaussian regularized sliced Wasserstein distance in
[
4
, Section 2]
.

Definition 4.2

(Gaussian regularized sliced Wasserstein distance)
.

For any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\theta\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|x|=1\}
, we define the mapping
P_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by
P_{\theta}(x):=\theta^{\top}x
and the pushforward measure
\mu_{\theta}:=P_{\theta}\sharp\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. For any
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the sliced Wasserstein distance is defined by

SW_{2}(\mu,\nu):=\left\{\int_{\mathcal{S}^{d-1}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big[$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big[$}}}}}\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu_{\theta},\nu_{\theta})\mathclose{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big]$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{285200}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big]$}}}}}}^{2}d\theta\right\}^{1/2},

where the integration is taken with respect to the standard spherical measure on
\mathcal{S}^{d-1}
. Moreover, the Gaussian regularized sliced Wasserstein distance is defined by

SW_{2}^{\sigma}(\mu,\nu):=SW_{2}(\mu^{\sigma},\nu^{\sigma}),

where
\mu^{\sigma}:=\mu*\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the normal distribution with variance
\sigma^{2}I_{d}
for
\sigma\in(0,\infty)
. By abuse of notation,
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}(y)dy
also denotes the
1
-dimensional normal distribution with mean 0 and variance
\sigma^{2}
. We have that
\mu^{\sigma}_{\theta}:=(\mu^{\sigma})_{\theta}=

(\mu*\mathcal{N}_{\sigma})_{\theta}=\mu_{\theta}*\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}=(\mu_{\theta})^{\sigma}
, where the first Gaussian is
d
-dimensional and the second one is
1
-dimensional.

Denoting the cumulative distribution function of
\mu
by
F_{\mu}
, it is well known that (for example, see
[
42
, Theorem 2.18]
) in the one-dimensional case, the optimal transport map from
\mu_{\theta}^{\sigma}
to
\nu_{\theta}^{\sigma}
is given by

\displaystyle T_{\theta,\mu}^{\sigma}(x):=F_{\nu_{\theta}^{\sigma}}^{-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big($}}}}}F_{\mu_{\theta}^{\sigma}}(x)\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big)$}}}}},

which satisfies
\big[\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu_{\theta}^{\sigma},\nu_{\theta}^{\sigma})\bigr]^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{2}\big|x-T_{\theta,\mu}^{\sigma}(x)\bigr|^{2}\mu_{\theta}^{\sigma}(dx)
. Note that because it is the optimal transport map from
\mu_{\theta}^{\sigma}
to
\nu_{\theta}^{\sigma}
, for any
X\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\mathcal{L}(X)=\mu
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big($}}}}}T_{\theta,\mu}^{\sigma}(\theta^{\top}(X+N_{\sigma}))\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big)$}}}}}=\nu_{\theta}^{\sigma},

(4.1)

where
N_{\sigma}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a normal random variable independent of
X
, following the distribution
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}
.

The following lemma is taken from
[
4
, Lemmas 2.1]
.

Lemma 4.3
.

For any
\sigma>0
,
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),SW_{2}^{\sigma})
is a complete metric space, and it is equal to
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathcal{W}_{2})
as a topological space.

In order to establish the regularity of the derivatives of this metric, we require an estimate on the optimal transport map as outlined below.

Lemma 4.4
.

Fix
\sigma>0
. Let
\mathcal{V}
,
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be any compact sets such that all measures in
\mathcal{V}
,
\mathcal{U}
are of the form
\mu*\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}
for some
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Let
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be any compact set. There is a positive constant
C_{\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{X},\sigma}
depending only on
\mathcal{U}
,
\mathcal{V}
,
\mathcal{X}
and
\sigma
such that

\displaystyle|T_{\theta,\mu}^{\sigma}(z)|\exp\left(\dfrac{-(z-\theta^{\top}x)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)\leq C_{\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{X},\sigma}\left\{\exp\left(\dfrac{-z^{2}}{4\sigma^{2}}\right)\vee\left[\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big($}}}}}|z|^{1/2}+1\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big)$}}}}}\exp\left(\frac{-z^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}}\right)\right]\right\},

for all
\nu^{\sigma}\in\mathcal{V}
,
\mu^{\sigma}\in\mathcal{U}
,
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
z\in\mathbb{R}
and
\theta\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1}
.

Proof.

The values of the constants in this proof may change from line to line in this proof, but they still depend on the same set of parameters as mentioned. First of all, we show that the map
\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{S}^{d-1}\ni(\mu^{\sigma},\theta)\mapsto\mu_{\theta}^{\sigma}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is continuous. Let
(\mu_{n}^{\sigma},\theta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence converging to
(\mu^{\sigma},\theta)
. For a
L^{2}
random variable
X
such that
\mathcal{L}(X)=\mu^{\sigma}
, it yields that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu_{n,\theta_{n}}^{\sigma},\mu_{\theta}^{\sigma})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu_{n,\theta_{n}}^{\sigma},\mu_{\theta_{n}}^{\sigma})+\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu_{\theta_{n}}^{\sigma},\mu_{\theta}^{\sigma})
